Found and fixed one real math bug and four drift bugs: (1) the BMTRE Accuracy Score's bottom-catch component graded `bottomCatch` against a flat ceiling of 50, but Bill's ratings skew high enough that only ~30 titles in the whole 533-title eval set are genuine dislikes (myRating<=5) — a perfect model could never catch more than ~30, silently capping this component's achievable max at 60/100 regardless of model quality; added `bottomPossible` (`Math.min(50, totalDisliked)`) to `computeEvalMetrics()` and graded against that instead, the same fix `meanBaselineMae` already gives the MAE component — real result: bottom-catch reads a fairer 26.7% (8/30) instead of a misleadingly harsh 16% (8/50), moving the overall BMTRE Accuracy Score 65->66 (a small honest correction, not score inflation). (2) `franchise-signal-unused`'s "111 of N titles have `belongsToCollection`" was hardcoded and had drifted (real live count: 146, since more titles were enriched after that number was written) — made it compute live like every other finding in the file already does. (3) `no-diversity-reranking`'s live "X of 20 are tagged Drama" clause was immediately followed by a second, hardcoded "and all 20 of 20 are tagged Drama" — true today by coincidence but a latent self-contradiction risk the moment the real number changed — replaced with a second genuinely-live clause reporting the #2 genre instead. (4) `dropped-show-signal`'s "only 1 clear case" was hardcoded prose sitting next to a correctly-interpolated number elsewhere in the same finding — now interpolates throughout. (5) The `awards` field's quality-bar note was backwards ("0 is a legitimate answer for most titles") when live data shows the opposite (~80% of OMDb-enriched titles have some real recognition, since `totalWins`/`totalNominations` set a low bar and Bill's library skews toward well-regarded content) — corrected to match reality. Also updated a stale code comment in `computeMetadataQuality()` that still cited "18 of 22" open findings from before this session's cover-images fix flipped one to Resolved, rewording it to describe the mechanism (reads live via the `findings` param) rather than embedding a number that will drift again. **Everything else checked and left alone**: the Metadata & Engine Quality score (58/100) and its 15/15/10/10/50 component weighting, the FIELD_REGISTRY quality thresholds (including re-verifying the audience-score "both RT and Metacritic" bar against a real RT/MC-overlap breakdown — 322 of 376 titles with any critic score have both, so the bar isn't unreasonably strict), and 19 of 22 findings' ratings/severities all held up against live data with no changes needed — confirmed by recomputing every live-checkable claim, not spot-checking only the ones that looked suspicious. Verified end-to-end with a local server + Playwright at desktop and 375px mobile: both dials and all 22 finding cards render the corrected numbers, the CLI eval (`trakt/scripts/eval.js`) reports the new fair bottom-catch ceiling, no console errors, no horizontal overflow. Merged and pushed to both the feature branch and `main`. **Continued same session — Bill: "If any field is less than 90% populated or has data quality of less than 90%, that should be added as an improvement idea."** New `computeFieldQualityFindings()` generates one Improvement Opportunity card per FIELD_REGISTRY field failing either bar, computed live every render off the same `fieldStats` the Field Population & Quality table already renders — so a field crossing back above 90% drops off the list automatically, the same self-resolving pattern every other live-checked finding in this file follows, and a field is never "manually marked resolved." Four fields are under the bar today and got real, hand-researched writeups rather than generic boilerplate: **imdbId** (71.1% populated) — root-caused by source: library/watchlist are 100% (real Trakt data), the entire gap is 301 of 476 discovered candidates missing an id, which only get one via `enrich_tmdb.py`'s `external_ids` append — also the hard precondition for the next two fields, since OMDb eligibility requires a known IMDb id first. **genres** (82.5% quality, needs 2+ tags) — 130 of 742 titles carry only 1 genre tag, confirmed via a prior session's spot-check (30 Rock, both Anchorman movies) as real, current TMDB tagging, not a pipeline bug. **audienceScore** (37.6%/31.5%) — the real OMDb RT/MC coverage gap already being partially addressed by this session's in-flight Metacritic scraper backfill. **awards** (79.7% quality) — a genuine ~1-in-5 minority with no real recognition on record, correctly framed as real data rather than a gap. Any field that dips below 90% in a future session without a hand-written entry here falls through to a generic finding built from its own FIELD_REGISTRY `note` field, so nothing is silently unlisted just because it wasn't anticipated. **Caught and fixed a real consistency bug before shipping**: the first draft computed the imdbId source-breakdown and genre single/multi counts by summing the three raw source lists and scanning `enrichedMetadata.json` directly — which double-counts any title present in more than one source list and includes stale/pruned entries no longer in the active title set, producing numbers that didn't add up to the same eligible totals the adjacent `fieldStats` percentages describe (caught via a live Playwright consistency check before commit: the breakdown said "335 single + 1158 multi" against an eligible population of 742 — impossible). Fixed by building one deduped-by-titleKey, source-priority-ordered title list mirroring `computeFieldQuality()`'s own dedup logic exactly, and computing every breakdown from that — re-verified live afterward: "130 single + 612 multi = 742" now matches the eligible total exactly. Verified end-to-end with a local server + Playwright at desktop and 375px mobile: all 4 new cards render with correct, internally-consistent numbers, correct severities/ratings, no console errors, no horizontal overflow. Metadata & Engine Quality score moved 58->49 as an honest, expected consequence of surfacing 4 more real open findings — the score is designed to move with the open-finding count, not a bug. Merged and pushed to both the feature branch and `main`. **Continued same session — checked in on the 450-title Metacritic scrape batch (run `32586693411`).** It completed successfully (~94 min), landing 447 real entries in `trakt/data/scrapedShowRatings.json` (up from 25) — merged cleanly (fast-forward, no conflicts). Spot-checked the raw data before trusting it: confirmed the handful of entries still carrying a leftover `rottenTomatoes` value from an earlier same-session test run (before `SCRAPE_RT` was set to `False`) are harmless, since `mergeScrapedShowRatings()` only ever reads `.metacritic` from this file; also confirmed 0 titles are stuck in a "has RT, still missing MC" state that `is_cached_done()`'s either-field check could otherwise trap forever. Real effect: `audienceScore` populated jumped 37.6%->90.4%, crossing the 90% bar its field-quality finding (added earlier this session) checks against — which immediately exposed a real drift bug in that finding's own hardcoded "below the 90% bar on both" phrasing, now self-contradictory since population alone had cleared the bar. Fixed by computing which bar(s) are actually failing live (population vs. quality) instead of assuming both, and rewrote the explanation for why quality (31.5%, needs BOTH RT and MC) stays low despite the fix: Rotten Tomatoes only ever comes from OMDb itself (the scraper is Metacritic-only by design), and only 338 of 997 OMDb-cached titles have any RT score at all — a real structural ceiling more Metacritic scraping can't close on its own. Threaded `omdbMeta` through `computeFieldQualityFindings()` to compute this live. Verified end-to-end with Playwright at desktop and 375px mobile: corrected, self-consistent text renders, all 26 cards intact, no console errors, no overflow. The other 3 field-quality findings (imdbId, genres, awards) are unaffected by this scrape and unchanged. Merged and pushed to both the feature branch and `main`. **Continued same session — Bill: "Run a full data quality analysis of MC data."** Ran a real audit of `trakt/data/scrapedShowRatings.json` (447 entries): structural checks first (no malformed values, no out-of-range scores, no duplicate `mcUrl` across different titles, a plausible score distribution) came back clean, then real accuracy verification against outside sources — via WebSearch, not assumed — found a genuine, confirmed bug. `scrape_metacritic()`'s direct-URL-slug guess (the only path that has ever actually worked, per this file's own prior investigation) has no confirmation step at all: it trusts whatever `aggregateRating` it finds on whatever page the guessed URL happens to resolve to. Confirmed 9 wrong entries via real cross-checks, two distinct failure modes: **unreleased titles** (Cupertino/Neagley/Crystal Lake, each showing a fabricated 93-97 "critic score" despite zero aired episodes) and **title collisions** (Lost in Space scored 93 from the wrong 1965-original page vs. its real 2018 reboot's actual 58; Perry Mason 96 vs. real 68; The Agency 97 vs. a real "Generally Favorable" band; Legends 59 vs. real 75 — each because the guessed bare-slug URL is occupied by an older/different same-named work, and the real page lives at a year-suffixed URL the naive guess never reaches; plus Ambitions and Elway, both genuinely un-scored on Metacritic). All 9 corrected to null in the committed cache (verified: JSON stays valid, 447 entries preserved, exactly 9 lines changed). Fixed with two independent, unit-tested guards, both deliberately asymmetric — reject only on an explicit, positive conflict, never require a signal to be present, the same discipline `resolve_titles.py`'s `is_confident_match()` already established: `is_unreleased()` discards any score for a known future release date outright; the JSON-LD/regex parsing logic was extracted into its own pure, directly-testable `extract_metascore()` (plus `page_title_matches()`), which cross-checks the fetched page's own JSON-LD date field and `<title>` tag against the expected title/year before trusting anything scraped from it. **Caught and fixed a real bug in the fix's own first draft**: an initial version of `page_title_matches()` required a year to appear in the title for any normalized title under 15 characters — but a unit test replaying real confirmed-good matches (The Boys, Fleabag, Squid Game, Ambitions, none of which show a year in their real `<title>` since none of them actually collide with an older work) immediately caught it as wrong: it would have silently discarded a large share of genuinely correct short-title matches. Rewrote to only reject on an explicit, disagreeing year rather than requiring one's presence. 25 unit tests across `is_unreleased`/`page_title_matches`/`extract_metascore` verify both the confirmed-bad cases are now rejected and the confirmed-good cases (including short, no-year titles) still pass through unchanged. Added a new dashboard Improvement Opportunity finding documenting this, honestly scoped: the 9 confirmed cases are fixed, but the remaining ~365 already-cached values were not individually re-verified this session (that would mean hundreds of manual searches) — flagged a full re-scrape with the new guards as the thorough next step, not claimed as already done. Verified end-to-end with a local server + Playwright at desktop and 375px mobile: 27 improvement cards render correctly, no console errors, no horizontal overflow. Merged and pushed to both the feature branch and `main`. **Continued same session — while the full Metacritic re-scrape ran in the background, Bill asked "what else can we do while we're waiting? Let's improve the recommendations."** Picked the highest-verified-impact open finding already on the dashboard's own Improvement Opportunities list rather than guessing at a new one: `no-diversity-reranking` — a live check found the real top-8-by-score shows had 4 of 8 sharing one primary genre (Drama), with no anti-clustering pass anywhere in the pipeline. New `diversityRerank(scoredList, enrichedMeta, {windowSize, maxPerGenre})` in `engine.js`: a soft per-primary-genre cap within a display window — a title over the cap is deferred past titles that add real variety, not excluded, and the window backfills from the deferred queue if the pool genuinely lacks enough diversity to fill it (never shows fewer picks than requested). Deliberately applied only at the display layer (`renderRecPanel()`, the "Movies/Shows You'll Love" panels) — `rankAll()`/`rankRecommendations()` stay pure score-order for every other consumer that needs the true rank (the All Titles table, exports, `prune_candidate_pool.js`), and `computeEvalMetrics()`'s precision@k is completely unaffected by design (verified: 80/92/92/86 unchanged before and after). Unit-tested against synthetic data covering the cap, the all-one-genre backfill case, and empty/unenriched inputs. **Caught and fixed a real measurement bug while building the live before/after proof for the corresponding finding**: the first draft counted every genre a title carries, not just its primary one — since most of these shows are blended Crime+Drama titles that just alternate which TMDB lists first, that check showed "8 of 8 tagged Drama" both before AND after the fix, making a real improvement look like none at all. Fixed to count only `genres[0]`, the same definition `diversityRerank()` itself caps against — corrected live numbers now show a real, verified drop (4/8 before, capped at exactly 3/8 after). Updated the finding to severity "good" with this live proof. Verified end-to-end with a local server + Playwright at desktop and 375px mobile: both rec panels still render exactly 8 cards each, no console errors, no horizontal overflow. Merged and pushed to both the feature branch and `main`. **Continued same session — Bill: "Yes. Proceed on the highest impact item."** Implemented the franchise/sequel signal, the next-highest-verified-impact open finding: `belongsToCollection` was cached on 146 enriched movie titles (TMDB's collection concept is movie-only) but never contributed to `matchScore()`, despite 17 of Bill's real loved titles demonstrably following a franchise (Creed I+II, Deadpool 1+2, Sicario 1+2, both Anchorman films). New `idx.lovedCollections` index in `buildIndexes()` and `franchiseBonus()` in `engine.js` (+10 for one loved franchise entry, +3 per additional, capped at +15 — the same order of magnitude as the creator-match bonus); `reason()` now checks this FIRST, ahead of even a director/creator match. Measured before and after via `scripts/eval.js` per this project's standing discipline: precision@10/25/50 unchanged (80/92/92), precision@100 86->87 and MAE 20.69->20.54 — small, genuine gains. Verified live against real candidates: "Deadpool & Wolverine" now reads "You loved Deadpool and Deadpool 2 — this is another entry in the same Deadpool franchise." Updated the corresponding finding to severity "good" with this live proof. **Then checked in on the full Metacritic re-scrape** (GitHub Actions run `32602901778`, triggered earlier this session) — it completed successfully (~99 min) and correctly nulled the 3 unreleased-title cases (Cupertino/Neagley/Crystal Lake), confirming `is_unreleased()` works in the real world. **But an honest, important finding**: 6 of the 9 originally-confirmed-wrong titles (Elway, Ambitions, The Agency, Lost in Space, Perry Mason, Legends) came back with the *exact same wrong scores* as before the fix — confirmed the fixed code was genuinely present at the commit this run executed against (not a stale-checkout issue), so the `page_title_matches()`/`extract_metascore()` title/date-conflict guard itself simply isn't catching these in the real world. Most likely explanation, not yet confirmed (still no live page access to verify against): the guard is deliberately asymmetric — reject only on an explicit, positive conflict, never require one to be present, specifically so it wouldn't wrongly reject legitimate no-year matches like The Boys or Fleabag — and the real wrong pages apparently don't expose an explicit conflicting year in their `<title>` or JSON-LD either, leaving the guard with nothing to act on. `is_unreleased()`'s 3/3 real-world hit rate stands in sharp contrast to this guard's 0/6. Corrected all 6 back to null again rather than leave known-wrong data in place, and documented the honest gap directly in the fix commit rather than claiming victory — a more reliable disambiguator (cross-checking Metacritic's own IMDb link against our already-known `ids.imdb`, an exact-id match instead of fuzzy title/year text) is flagged as the likely next step for a future session, not guessed at further without a way to verify it. Verified end-to-end with a local server + Playwright at desktop and 375px mobile after merging everything together: `eval.js` shows a further genuine improvement now that 379 real Metacritic values are feeding `omdbSignal()` (precision@50 92->94, precision@100 87->89), 27 improvement cards render correctly, no console errors, no horizontal overflow. Merged and pushed to both the feature branch and `main`. **Continued same session — Bill: "Do both."** Tackled the scraper fix and the next recommendation-engine improvement in parallel. **Scraper fix**: built a harder disambiguator after the title/date guard's 0/6 real-world result. New `page_imdb_matches()` in `scrape_show_ratings.py` — every title reaching this script already has a known IMDb id (a precondition of OMDb eligibility), so cross-checking Metacritic's own IMDb reference (a generic `imdb.com/title/ttNNNNNNN` scan across the whole page, not one assumed markup location) against that id is an exact match, not fuzzy text. Wired in as the primary check in `extract_metascore()`: a confirmed mismatch rejects regardless of the title check, a confirmed match skips the title check entirely, and "no id found" falls back to the pre-existing check unchanged. Unit-tested (31 tests total) against synthetic reconstructions of all 6 real round-2 failures — every one now correctly rejected — plus every prior regression case. Honestly left at severity "warning" on the dashboard, not marked resolved: this sandbox still can't fetch metacritic.com to verify against a real page, so the next real scrape run is the actual test, same discipline as round 1's own real disproof. **Cast/actor-affinity signal**: `topCast` (93.4% populated) was cached but never scored. New `idx.lovedActors` index and `castBonus()` in `engine.js` — a small, tiered, capped-at-8 bonus (deliberately smaller than creator/franchise bonuses, since an actor is less determinative of a title's identity than its director); `reason()` checks it after creator/franchise matches, before the general similar-title network. Measured before and after via `scripts/eval.js`: precision@10 jumped 80%->90%, precision@100 89%->91%, MAE improved 21.27->20.21 — one of the larger single-change improvements measured for this engine so far, with precision@25/50 holding steady and nothing regressing. Verified live: 104 real current candidates get the bonus today, e.g. "BEEF" now reads "You've loved 1 title with Steven Yeun before." Updated both corresponding Improvement Opportunities findings live (cast signal -> "good" with this proof; the MC scraper finding rewritten to honestly narrate both rounds — the first fix, its real-world failure, and the second attempt — rather than just replacing round 1's claim with round 2's). Verified end-to-end with a local server + Playwright at desktop and 375px mobile: both rec panels still render 8 cards each, 27 improvement cards total, no console errors, no horizontal overflow. Merged and pushed to both the feature branch and `main`. **Continued same session — Bill: "Keep going. Address all the known issues."** Worked through the Improvement Opportunities list systematically rather than picking one item: 14 of 26 findings moved from open to `good` this pass, each verified live, not just marked done. **Code-only fixes**: `enrich_omdb.py`'s `get_json()` now returns `(data, status, error_body)`, mirroring the identical fix `enrich_tmdb.py` got earlier — the exact structural gap Session 51's Improvement Opportunities list had already flagged but never applied to this sibling script. `build_trakt_library.js`'s `titleKey()` had its incompatible `${type}:trakt:${ids.trakt}` fallback removed entirely (verified 0 titles used it before removing) — a title with no TMDB id now falls into the same existing "unresolvable, skip and warn" bucket a title with neither id already used, instead of getting a key format nothing else in the pipeline recognizes. `genreBonus()`'s tier thresholds carried a stale "provisional, recalibrate once real data exists" comment for weeks after that data existed — formally checked for the first time (Bill's real lovedGenres distribution spans all 5 tiers with no collapse) and the comment rewritten to match. **New derived data-shape fields**: `resolveSimilarTitles()` and `resolveSimilarDirectors()` in `engine.js` — the book side's `similarToTitles`/`similarToAuthors` equivalents, which never existed for BMTRE. Both resolve a title's `similarToIds`/`recommendedIds` citations to real names via a self-referential lookup against `enrichedMetadata.json` itself (no new fetch needed); directors require a real corroboration threshold (2+ resolved similar titles sharing a director) before counting, closing the actual design gap the finding had flagged. Both wired into `loadAllTitles.js`/`export_extract.js` as new CSV columns; deliberately display-only, not scoring signals. **A validated new scoring signal, with a caught-and-fixed regression**: `keywordBonus()` reads a new `idx.lovedKeywords` index — TMDB's free-form keywords, previously read nowhere except the re-edit filter, are the closest BMTRE equivalent to the book engine's theme vocabulary. A `KEYWORD_STOPLIST` filters out pure structural tags (sequel, aftercreditsstinger, miniseries). Cap size tuned against `scripts/eval.js`, not guessed: an initial cap of 4 improved MAE (20.21→18.55) but dropped precision@10 90%→80% — a real regression on the metric CLAUDE.md says never trade away for MAE — halved to a cap of 2, which held precision@10/25/50/100 exactly unchanged while still improving MAE (20.21→19.34). **An attempted signal that was reverted, not shipped**: tried using TMDB's `status` field ("Returning Series"/"In Production") to fix the show-recency gap (an old show still producing new content scored as "old" as one that ended years ago) — a flat +2, then +1, credit both measurably hurt `eval.js` (precision@10 90%→80%, precision@100 91%→86%, MAE got worse not better) and was reverted; documented as a real, now evidence-backed open gap rather than an unexplored idea, with a specific hypothesis for why a flat bonus across ~17.8% of all shows is too blunt an instrument. **Real backend data work**: triggered a full `REFRESH_ALL` TMDB re-enrichment pass (1,493→1,793 cached entries, picking up newly-discovered candidates along the way) which closed the imdbId gap that was blocking OMDb eligibility for discovered candidates (candidatePool imdbId population 63.2%→96.9%, 301→15 missing) — the corresponding `field-quality-imdbId` finding self-resolved. Triggered a real OMDb enrichment batch (900) once the new imdbIds existed, closing `field-quality-omdbRecord` too. Re-ran `prune_candidate_pool.js` for real after the refresh surfaced newly-enriched re-edit/non-English candidates that had accumulated past the last prune — pool back to a clean 100/100, resolving `pool-cap-waste`. **Verified the Metacritic scraper's round-2 IMDb-id fix in production, and found a 3rd bug**: cleared the 9 previously-corrected cache entries to force a real re-scrape (their `checkedAt` was still inside the 14-day retry cooldown) — confirmed 7 of 9 now correctly return `null` (Cupertino, Neagley, Crystal Lake, The Agency, Lost in Space, Perry Mason, Legends), a genuine, live-verified fix. The remaining 2 (Elway, Ambitions) turned out to be a different bug: WebSearch confirmed the guessed URLs are the correct page (matching IMDb id, so round 2's guard correctly passes them), but neither title has any real critic reviews on Metacritic — the JSON-LD `aggregateRating` block itself carried a placeholder rating with nothing backing it. Fixed with a 3rd guard in `extract_metascore()`: reject when `ratingCount`/`reviewCount` is explicitly present and zero, same asymmetric rule (never reject on an absent signal) as every other guard here. Unit-tested against both the real bug case and a no-count-field case; both entries corrected to null; scanned the remaining 14 scores ≥90 in the cache and found only real, well-known critically-acclaimed titles, no further false positives — round 3 itself not yet re-verified against a live page. **Left honestly open, with documented reasons rather than silently skipped**: `dismissal-generalization` (all 18 real dismissals are content-shape reasons like too_urban/too_old, already explicitly excluded from generalization after Session 53 found they'd misfire against Bill's own top genres — no safe creator/style-dislike signal exists yet to generalize from); `plays-field-semantic-trap`/`dropped-show-signal` (informational by design, not bugs); `categories`/`themes`/`tones-field-missing`/`genre-subgenre-split-missing` (genuine multi-session LLM-tagging projects, the same scale as the book side's Session 16 tone-vocabulary redesign); `field-quality-genres`/`field-quality-audienceScore`/`field-quality-awards` (confirmed real TMDB/OMDb data ceilings, not pipeline bugs). **Verified throughout**: `scripts/eval.js` re-run after every scoring-relevant change (caught the keyword-signal regression and the recency-signal regression before either shipped); full Playwright regression (desktop + 375px mobile, console errors, horizontal overflow) after every dashboard change; the `window.__debug` introspection hook added for live verification was removed before every commit. Merged 5 more automated commits that landed on `main` mid-session (the triggered TMDB refresh, OMDb batch, and Metacritic scrape runs themselves, plus routine daily jobs) — all clean merges. **Continued same session — Bill: "keep going."** Closed the categories-field-missing finding as redundant with the already-shipped keywords work (the book side's `categories` field is Google Books' own free-form, auto-populated category strings with no curation — structurally identical to what TMDB's `keywords` field already is for BMTRE, and now also a real scoring signal via `keywordBonus()`, a stronger role than `categories` plays on the book side). Re-verified the Metacritic scraper's round-2 IMDb-id fix a second time (cleared the Elway/Ambitions cache entries again, triggered a real 500-title re-scrape) and found round 3's `ratingCount=0` guard genuinely did NOT work in production — both titles came back with the exact same wrong 98/97 scores, since their JSON-LD apparently omits `ratingCount` entirely rather than stating zero, making the guard's own asymmetric design a no-op for this case. Investigated the full 500-title cache afterward and found a real, discoverable pattern instead: every score ≥90 landing on exactly 97 or 98 was confirmed fabricated via WebSearch (Elway, Ambitions, plus 2 more caught by the same scan — Thieves' Highway, Kyle XY — 4 for 4, zero real critic reviews each), while every genuinely-scored acclaimed title in the same cache lands at 90-95, never exactly 97 or 98. Shipped round 4: reject a Metascore of exactly 97 or 98 when there's no real `ratingCount` to justify it, unit-tested against both confirmed-bad cases and every other score 90-100 to confirm nothing legitimate gets wrongly rejected. All 4 entries corrected to null; full cache rescan confirms 0 remaining 97s or 98s. **Bill then approved starting the themes/tones/genre-subgenre-split taxonomy work** (offered via AskUserQuestion once every bounded technical fix was exhausted, since unlike the rest of this session's fixes this one needed real taste/design input the same way the book side's Session 16 tone redesign needed Bill's explicit brief) — planned via `EnterPlanMode` given the scale, with a background research agent extracting the book side's exact theme/tone mechanics as ground truth first (confirmed the real 24-word `tag_with_haiku.py` tone vocabulary, and that the actual Session 16 migration classifier was never committed to git — only documented in CLAUDE.md's own prose). **Shipped a real, validated classifier**: new `inferSubgenres()`/`inferTones()` in `engine.js` — deterministic, keyword-driven, computed live from each title's real TMDB keywords (98.7% populated), deliberately NOT persisted to `enrichedMetadata.json` or any data file, a departure from the book side's persisted-array design chosen specifically because it sidesteps that design's entire drift-prevention burden (4 files kept in sync by hand, a disjointness assertion, two drift-detection functions) — a value returned here can only ever be a literal key of `SUBGENRE_KEYWORDS`/`TONE_KEYWORDS`, so non-canonical drift has no code path to occur through. 21 subgenres, 14 tones, every keyword verified present in the real dataset before inclusion (not assumed). Real distribution checked before shipping: no subgenre tag exceeds 14.7% (under the book side's 15% cap precedent). **Two real false positives caught via hand-spot-checking ~20 real titles** (the same manual-audit discipline Session 16c/17 used) and fixed before shipping, not after: 'based on comic' wrongly tagged "300" (a historical war epic based on a graphic novel, not a superhero film) as superhero; bare decade-marker keywords wrongly tagged "Anchorman: The Legend of Ron Burgundy" (a comedy merely set in the 1970s) as historical — both fixed by narrowing the trigger keywords, trading a little recall for real precision. Tone coverage is honestly lower (18.1%) than subgenre coverage (64.6%) — several plausible mood words ("heartwarming", "bleak", "ensemble", "fast-paced") were checked against the real dataset and returned zero matches anywhere, so were dropped from the vocabulary rather than shipped as permanently-empty tags. Wired into `loadAllTitles.js`/`export_extract.js` as new `subgenres`/`tones` CSV columns, and into the Field Population & Quality table via two new `FIELD_REGISTRY` entries (both correctly auto-flagged below the 90% bar, since that's real and honest). Closed 3 findings at once (`themes-field-missing`, `tones-field-missing`, `genre-subgenre-split-missing`) since for BMTRE — unlike the book side, where categories/themes are genuinely separate fields — a subgenre tag and a subject-matter theme tag are the same kind of thing sitting beneath the same blunt genre taxonomy. Deliberately display/audit-only, not wired into `matchScore()` — a real scoring weight needs the same `eval.js`-gated validation `keywordBonus()` went through earlier this session, flagged as a real next step but not bundled into this change. `eval.js` confirms zero change throughout (p10=90/p25=92/p50=92/p100=86, unchanged, as expected for a display-only addition); full Playwright regression clean at every step (desktop + 375px mobile, no console errors, no overflow, both new field-table rows and CSV columns verified with real data). Real page-load performance bug reported live ("the webpage isn't loading again" / "it did but it takes a very long time"): root-caused to `computeEvalMetrics()`'s leave-one-out loop rebuilding a full TF-IDF description model from scratch on every excluded title (expensive, and provably unnecessary — excluding a non-loved title can't change the loved-title corpus the model is built from). Hit a real, subtle bug getting the non-blocking part right: an `async` function still runs synchronously up to its *first* `await`, so calling the now-async function via `.then()` without an immediate yield at its own top meant it still blocked through the descModel build and first several titles before ever yielding — fixed by adding `await new Promise(r => setTimeout(r, 0));` as the literal first line inside the function. Consolidated via the same proven pattern: remapped the fragmented tail onto canonical buckets (139→17 canonical values) plus two new `TONE_KEYWORDS` entries (`atmospheric`, `fast-paced`) checked for zero vocabulary-collision via `findTaxonomyCollisions()` before adding. Verified via `scripts/eval.js`: no regression. **Two Q&A-only exchanges, no code changes**: explained exactly how `Genres (raw TMDB)` (still load-bearing — feeds `inferGenre()`'s deterministic-fallback tier and `normalizeGenre()`) differs from the clean single-valued `Genre` field (what `genreBonus()`/`lovedGenres` actually score against), with exact `engine.js` line references; and, on request, printed the live `genreBonus()` tier table plus real current per-genre weights from `idx.lovedGenres`. (2) Even fully asymmetric, penalizing every negative delta however small still regressed precision@25 identically across every magnitude tested (0.25 to 4) — traced to the *same* clamp-tie mechanism from the other direction: mild, low-confidence negatives like action (-0.18, n=29) or science-fiction (-0.37, n=43) knocked a couple of personally-loved candidates (Daredevil: Born Again, Devs) a few points below the 100 ceiling, which reshuffled which *other* already-maxed candidates the confidence-score tiebreak surfaced in the visible top 25 (Big Little Lies, Lioness, both 7/10) — fixed with a -0.5 deadzone so only genres Bill has rated sizably below his own average (mystery -0.53, biography -0.70, horror -1.47) ever take a penalty. Fixed by adding an explicit hand-written `shortWindow` field to every entry instead of pattern-matching prose after the fact — removes the ambiguity at the source rather than trying to disambiguate it algorithmically. Went looking for something genuinely substantive rather than another marginal field-quality gap: wrote a scratch investigation script replicating `computeEvalMetrics()`'s real leave-one-out predictions to check whether the model's long-standing "MAE is worse than the naive mean-baseline" caveat — repeated, unfixed, across dozens of prior session-log entries — was actually fixable. **Found and fixed a real, structural gap**: `matchScore()` was only ever designed to rank candidates correctly relative to each other, never to land on an absolute 0-100 scale matching `myRating*10` directly — real signal exists (Pearson correlation 0.35 with actual rating; mean predicted climbs cleanly from 57 at myRating=4 to 83 at myRating=9), the problem was scale, not content. A simple linear recalibration (`actual ≈ 0.298 × predicted + 55.8`) fixes exactly that — validated via 5-fold cross-validation before shipping (not just fit-and-trust): held-out calibrated MAE 10.50 vs. 14.65 uncalibrated vs. 11.03 naive baseline, stable across all 5 folds (slope 0.276-0.342, intercept 52.7-57.6 — a real, generalizing correction). New `RATING_CALIBRATION`/`calibrateScore()` in `engine.js`, a new `calibratedMae` field on `computeEvalMetrics()`'s return, wired into `computeBMTREAccuracy()`'s Rating Accuracy component (Rating Accuracy moved ~34→~53/100) and both the dashboard footnote and `trakt/scripts/eval.js`'s CLI output. Deliberately order-preserving by construction (a positive-slope affine transform can't change which of two titles ranks higher) and scoped only to the evaluation/accuracy-reporting layer — the live displayed "score" on rec cards/tables/deep-dive is completely untouched, confirmed via `eval.js`'s precision@10/25/50/100 being bit-for-bit identical before and after (90/96/98/91). **Found and fixed a second, independent, previously-invisible bug while verifying the first fix live**: the dashboard's own BMTRE Accuracy dial (`quality.js`'s `load()`) called `computeEvalMetrics(library, enrichedMeta, feedback, omdbMeta)` — only 4 of 6 parameters, silently defaulting `llmTags`/`reviewedTags` to `{}` even though both are already in scope two lines above (used correctly by the very next `rankAll()` call) — meaning the dashboard's own "official" accuracy numbers had been silently diverging from the CLI's for as long as this dial has existed: precision@10 showed 100.0% live vs. the CLI's real 90.0%, an 11-point gap from missing tone/subgenre/reviewed-tag signal. One-line fix (pass the already-loaded values through); verified live afterward that the dial's numbers now match the CLI exactly (90/96/98/91). Both fixes added as new, `severity: 'good'` Improvement Opportunities findings (`rating-score-calibration`, `eval-metrics-missing-args-bug`) with the real derivation numbers, per this dashboard's established "document the fix as a resolved finding" convention — correctly hidden from the visible open-findings list (Bill's own prior "once resolved, remove it" rule) while still counting toward the resolved total (23→25) and the Metadata & Engine Quality dial's known-issues component. Verified end-to-end: `node --check` clean on `engine.js`/`quality.js`/`scripts/eval.js`; live Playwright confirmed the accuracy dial renders the corrected component scores and footnote text, the resolved-count note reads "25," no console errors, no horizontal overflow at desktop or 375px mobile. **Continued, follow-up request**: Bill: "think harder and find new improvement ideas; focus mainly on how you can improve the Trakt recommendation engine." Used the already-shipped `scoreBreakdown()` (a verified row-by-row decomposition of `matchScorePair()`) to root-cause the actual worst prediction misses/underrates from `eval.js`'s own output, rather than brainstorm abstractly. **One hypothesis was investigated, found wrong, and corrected by Bill directly, not silently dropped**: 5 of 8 worst-underrated titles (Wedding Crashers, Dumb and Dumber, How to Lose a Guy in 10 Days, Walk the Line — all real 9-10/10s) were pre-2010 with steep negative `recencyBonusMovie()` penalties, and Bill's own rated-title mean climbed cleanly from 7.46 (2020s) to 9.80 (pre-2000) — looked like the recency curve was fighting a real revealed preference for older content. Bill caught the flawed premise immediately: "I only added older movies that I loved. I add all new movies even if I don't like them." Verified against real data rather than just accepting the correction on faith: confirmed 0 of 35 disliked (myRating≤5) titles predate 2000 and only 1 predates 2010 — exactly matching a pure selection-bias explanation, not a genuine taste signal. **Reframed into the real, corrected finding** (`library-recency-selection-bias`): every `myRating`-derived preference signal (`genreProfile`/`toneProfile`/`creatorRatingWeight`) structurally cannot learn "Bill dislikes X in an old title" for pre-2000-ish content, since no negative example exists in the training data — and this also means Session 52's explicit, twice-stated recency-penalty ask is plausibly doing necessary corrective work no organic signal could do alone, not something this pattern argues against. Two other hypotheses were tested and found NOT to be real problems (both disclosed honestly rather than presented as fixes): a symmetric (not just negative) `genreSignal()` moved `eval.js` MAE by a noise-level 0.09 and left precision identical, since comedy's real self-rating delta (+0.33) sits below the signal's own 0.5 deadzone regardless of sign; and the title-similarity signal (forward+reverse match), despite dominating several of the worst misses' point totals, turned out to have a real, clean, monotonic correlation with actual rating (0.267, bucket means climbing from 7.36 to 8.36) — a normal, expected miss rate for a genuinely-working-but-imperfect signal, not a broken one. **Three new findings added to the Improvement Opportunities list, each grounded in real numbers, none silently fixed** (Bill: "it's ok if you don't have a solution, just find the problems"): `library-recency-selection-bias` (above); `weak-keyword-desc-signal-correlation` — a real leave-one-out correlation sweep of every scored signal's own point contribution against actual rating (community 0.349, forward-similar 0.240, creator 0.181, reverse-similar 0.152, genre 0.148, cast 0.124, keyword 0.074, description-similarity 0.070 — the bottom two close to noise level, clearly explained as a diagnostic flag rather than proof either signal is worthless, since `description-similarity-signal-missing`'s own prior eval.js sweep already validated real combined-model value); `diversity-rerank-no-creator-cap` — `diversityRerank()` caps genre repetition per display window but has no equivalent cap on director/creator, verified live against today's real top-20 pool (worst current repeat: James Gunn ×2, not an active problem today but a real, unguarded structural gap). Confirmed neither TMDB nor OMDb has any such field — built one from three real, independently-verified signals instead. **Format** (`isPrestigeFormat()`): `numberOfEpisodes/numberOfSeasons` (average episodes PER SEASON, not raw total) so a genuine anthology series (True Detective, The White Lotus — a new self-contained ~8-episode story each season) correctly counts, not just a one-and-done limited series. A >=4-episode floor avoids flagging a barely-started new show. TMDB's own free-form `miniseries` keyword (present on 7 of the 15 known examples, absent on the other 8 including The Westies itself — a real TMDB-side classification gap) folded in as corroboration, never a requirement, since it's demonstrably incomplete. **Critic acclaim + awards**: reused the already-existing, already-validated `criticScore()`/`awardsScore()` rather than hand-rolling new field access (caught a real bug in my own throwaway diagnostic script first — `omdbMetadata.json`'s `awards` fields are nested under `.awards`, not flat, so an ad hoc query read them all as 0/undefined; caught before it went into any real finding or code). **Star power**: real TMDB person-level `popularity` (Bill's own named example, J.K. Simmons) — confirmed neither `enrich_person.py` nor `personMetadata.json` (2,500 people cached) captured this field at all, though it's already present in the same `/person/{id}` response already being fetched for birthday/gender. Extended `enrich_person.py` to capture it, added a `REFRESH_ALL` mode (mirroring `enrich_tmdb.py`'s established pattern) since all 2,500 cached people predate the field, added a matching `refresh_all` workflow_dispatch input, bumped the workflow timeout 30→45 min (real math: ~4,867 unique cast people × 0.3s delay ≈ 24+ min). Verified live via Playwright: The Westies correctly shows Prestige=40 (pure format-only score — not yet critic-rated, star-power not yet calibrated), a real show (Bodyguard) already clears the 50-point badge threshold using existing critic/awards data alone, no console errors, no horizontal overflow at desktop or 375px mobile. **Known, clearly-flagged incompleteness, not silently shipped**: `STAR_POWER_REFERENCE` (J.K. Simmons' real TMDB popularity, needed to calibrate the star-power component's scale) is a TEMP placeholder (10) pending the real fetch, which was still `in_progress` after ~30+ minutes when a Stop-hook git-check required committing the working tree — committed and pushed as an explicitly-labeled in-progress checkpoint (code is syntactically valid and degrades safely either way: format+critic+awards, the majority of the score, are already fully real and verified; only the star-power adjustment is provisionally scaled) rather than either blocking on an indefinite wait or discarding real, working progress. Follow-up needed once the fetch completes: replace the placeholder with J.K. Simmons' real popularity score and re-verify. **Continued — the fetch completed for real** (run `33803981613`, ~31 min, all 4,867 people). J.K. Simmons' real TMDB popularity: 9.988, landing right at the 99th percentile of the full distribution (p99=10.75, median=1.99, mean=2.62) — genuinely "a known star," not an arbitrary number close to the placeholder's coincidental 10 by chance. `STAR_POWER_REFERENCE` replaced with the real, verified value. **Redesigned the star-power term while calibrating it against the real distribution**, catching a real design flaw before shipping: the original linear-ratio-against-the-99th-percentile formula would have floored almost the ENTIRE cast distribution at the same penalty (the median actor sits at only ~20% of J.K. Simmons' score), and allowed a penalty for lacking star power at all — wrong, since Bill's own framing was "maybe a big star," an additional positive signal, not a requirement, and plenty of real acclaimed limited series lean on strong character actors over A-listers. Rebuilt as positive-only, capped at +15, scaling smoothly across the real distribution rather than collapsing most of it to a floor. Verified live: The Westies scores 48 (format + real J.K. Simmons star power, honestly below the 50-point badge line since it has no critic-score data yet, being brand new — not artificially inflated to make the badge fire), Chernobyl/Mare of Easttown/The Queen's Gambit/The Night Of all land 78-79, and — the strongest validation, since it was never hand-picked as a test case — the single highest-scoring show dataset-wide is **Lonesome Dove** (81), a genuinely famous, Emmy-winning 1989 miniseries the formula found entirely on its own. 3 real shows (Off Campus, Bodyguard, Power Book IV: Force) now clear the rec-card badge threshold. Verified end-to-end: `node --check` clean, `node trakt/scripts/eval.js` unaffected (p10/p25/p50/p100 = 90/96/98/91, unchanged — display-only feature, no `matchScore()` touched), live Playwright at desktop and 375px mobile confirmed the All Titles table/rec-card badges/sort-by-Prestige all render correctly with the final real numbers, no console errors, no horizontal overflow. **Continued, same-turn request**: Bill: "look for another improvement idea that is totally out of the blue and might not work." Investigated whether *when* Bill watches something (day of week, month) predicts anything — untouched ground across 54+ sessions of engine work. The October-horror/Halloween-mood hypothesis was cleanly disproven (October horror-watching is slightly *below* an even distribution, not above). But a real, striking, unexplained pattern surfaced: 77.6% of rated movies (149 of 192) were watched on a Friday specifically — checked hard for the boring explanation (a bulk-import artifact) and ruled it out: those 149 span 135 distinct Friday dates, not a handful of backfill dates, and the pattern persists at 80-92% in most years across three real decades (1994-2026), not concentrated in one import period. Rated shows do NOT show the same Friday skew (Monday/Thursday-heaviest instead), which argues against a uniform Plex-server-scheduling artifact (that would hit both types equally) but doesn't fully resolve movies' case either way. Reported to Bill as a genuinely open, unresolved finding — could be a real "Friday movie night" habit worth building a feature around (a more prominent Friday-timed pick), or an artifact of how this specific Plex setup logs movie-watch dates specifically; deliberately not built into anything until Bill confirms which. |
| 60 (Sep 3 2026, claude code) | Bill's response to the Friday-watching finding ("I don't know how that would help the recommendation engine; any other ideas?") prompted a second "out of the blue, might not work" idea: a per-creator critic-trust gap, the same shape as the already-documented `flat-community-neutral-ignores-genre-bias` dead end but per-person instead of per-genre — does Bill rate a specific director/showrunner's other work higher or lower than critic consensus, on average? Checked real data first (`check_creator_trust.mjs`): a clean, real pattern exists both directions — Bill rates Adam McKay/Ridley Scott/Todd Phillips/Taylor Sheridan/David E. Kelley meaningfully above critic consensus (gaps +1.2 to +2.4, n=3-9 rated+critic-scored titles each), and Denis Villeneuve/Andy Muschietti/Noah Hawley meaningfully below (gaps -2.0 to -2.9) — with Villeneuve's negative gap directly matching the engine's single biggest current miss (Dune, predicted 71.4, actual 2/10). Built a scratch prototype rather than shipping on the strength of the anecdote alone (this project's standing discipline for exactly this shape of idea, since the structurally near-identical per-genre version already failed once): a `creatorCriticGap` map in a scratch copy of `buildIndexes()` (mean myRating minus mean critic score per creator, gated on the same >=3-rated-title trust floor `genreProfile`/`toneProfile` already use), applied as a capped symmetric adjustment in `baseSignals()`. Root-caused rather than left as a bare number: a live overlap check (`check_gap_overlap.mjs`) found 21 of 26 creators (81%) clearing the trust floor already trigger the existing `lovedCreators`/`creatorRatingWeight` director-match signal — so most of this signal's effect is a second, noisier vote stacked on an already-strong existing match, not new information; the 5 creators where it would be the only creator-identity signal are all negative-gap, meaning the "discover an under-the-radar creator Bill would love" upside case doesn't exist yet in this dataset. | 64 (Sep 3 2026, claude code) | Bill sent 4 real recommendations to act on and a direct instruction: "remember I'm not big into comic book movies, these should almost never appear as a top recommendation; re-evaluate your engine to see why this is happening." **Immediate asks**: dismissed Eurovision Song Contest: The Story of Fire Saga (`too_hokey`) and Zack Snyder's Justice League (`too_comicbooky`) via exact-title `feedbackData.json` exclusions; Batman Begins and The Suicide Squad (2021 — verified via real data check it's the James Gunn film, not the already-rated 2016 "Suicide Squad") both flagged `already_watched` with an honest note that this project only ever updates from a fresh Trakt export upload, never a live API call, so these stay excluded until Bill uploads one. **Root-caused the actual complaint rather than reaching for a genre penalty** — checked `scoreBreakdown()` for Zack Snyder's Justice League (93.0 raw) and found both its forward-match (+23.45, capped near the max) AND reverse-match (+12.00) credit traced back to the exact same single source: Deadpool. A mutual TMDB citation pair (Deadpool cites JL as similar, JL cites Deadpool back) was being counted as two independent votes instead of one. Checked how widespread this is before building anything: of 261 watchlist/candidate titles with both a forward and reverse loved-title match, 218 (84%) have the same title counted in both directions. Built a scratch fix in `buildIndexes()` (skip a reverse-match contribution when the candidate also cites that same loved title back) and confirmed it does exactly what it should on the motivating case — Justice League's raw score drops 91.2→83.6 with it on — but swept against `scripts/eval.js` across the whole pool and it regressed broadly (precision@10 100%→90%, precision@50 98%→94%, precision@100 91%→90%, MAE barely moved 14.64→14.59). Root cause of the regression: most mutual citations are genuine strong corroboration, not artifacts — e.g. "The Westies" cites and is cited back by 5 different loved crime dramas, a real deep match the blanket fix would have discounted right alongside the genuine Deadpool/Justice League anomaly. Documented as a new dashboard finding (`mutual-citation-double-count-tested`) rather than shipped — a correctly-diagnosed mechanism whose general-purpose fix isn't safe, same shape as the superhero-genre-penalty attempt a prior session already tried and reverted (that one specifically caught Deadpool/Deadpool 2 in the penalty and regressed precision@10 90%→80%). **Bill's immediate follow-up, still being worked when this entry was written**: "What are some other anomalies we should avoid using to find matches?" — i.e., which OTHER loved titles, like Deadpool, are genuine statistical outliers within a category Bill doesn't generally rate well, whose citation-network influence could be similarly misleading. Built a live outlier check (loved titles rated 9-10 whose own subgenre's average, excluding that title, sits 2.5+ points below the loved rating, with an 8+ title category floor to trust the average): real hits include Get Out (10/10, psychological-horror, category avg 6.22/n=9), The Killing (10/10, murder-mystery, avg 6.88/n=17), Pluribus (10/10, post-apocalyptic, avg 6.75/n=8), Watchmen (10/10, superhero, avg 7.27/n=15 — confirming superhero really is a genuine below-average category for Bill, not just a Deadpool coincidence), plus several coming-of-age (Pitch Perfect, Primo, Superbad) and romance (A Star Is Born, Obsession, This Is 40) titles. Not yet turned into a fix — given the mutual-citation test's own result this same session, a blanket per-category penalty for any of these is exactly the pattern already shown to backfire; the honest next step is checking whether each one is ALSO currently inflating real bad candidates in its category (the same specific diagnosis Zack Snyder's Justice League got) before considering any individual action, not applying a rule to the whole list at once.
